    Chapter 6: Book VI¶

    6.1 Book VI¶

    I The sixth book consists mainly of hymns of 3 verses. It is divided into thirteen anuvâka-groups, which have ten hymns each, except the third, seventh, eleventh, and twelfth groups, 4 which have eleven hymns each, and the thirteenth, which has eighteen. It thus contains one hundred and forty-two hymns: 4 and of these, one hundred and twenty-two have 3 verses each; twelve have 4 verses each ; and eight have 5 verses each. On account of the intrinsic interest, the variety, and the convenient length of the hymns of this book, they have been favorite subjects of translation and comment. Over half of them (79) have been translated by Ludwig in his Der Rigveda, vol. iii. (Die Mantra-btteratur); over half of them (74) also by Bloomfield in Sacred Books of the East, vol. xiii.; over a third (1—s0) by = Dr. Carl A. Florenz in Bezzenberger's Beitrâge aur Kunde der a Indogermanischen Spracken, vol. xii. 249—314; and nearly a quar- 5% ter (34) by Grill in his Hundert Lieder.) ¹ The hymns of 4 verses are hymns 16, 17, 38, 63, 76, 83, 84, 107, III, I21, I28, and — 130. The hymns of 5 verses are hymns 34, 108, 122, 123, I32, I33, I38, and r39. At the beginning of its treatment of this book, the Anukramaṇi calls it the " book of : % hymns of 3 verses " or Ścasṅktakângâa, and adds that "that number is the norm": tatra trcaprakytir itarâ vikytir iti. The possibilities of critical reduction to this ¹ “ norm " are frequently illustrated among the twenty hymns just cited. Thus the ¹ eo reduction may be effected by omission of some of the refrains and combination of the i 04 remainders, as in hymns 38, 107, I30 (this the commentator actually so reduces). HESŚ Again, the intrusions are sometimes indicated by the meter, as in hymns III, 123. Or, Ś again, they are indicated by their absence in other versions : thus hymns 16, 17, 34, i Ś 63, 83, 108, and r28 consist in fact of only 3 verses in the Pâippalâda text. — For the sequence of the books with reference to the normal lengths of their hymns, see the ¹ table on p. exlviii. See p. cli, end..

    [4 karvan. —sâvitram. âugnikam : I. 3p. pipṅikamadhyâ sâmnṅ jagari ; 2,3 pipṅlikamadâyE i puraugṇik.]

    Found also in Pâipp. xix. ; and in ACS. viii. I. 18 ; the first verse, too, is SV.i. 177. Used by Kâug. first (23. 2) in a rite for good fortune on building a house, with offering a second oblation ; then (50. 13) for success in traffic, with vi. 3-7, 59, 93, 107, 128, and

    281 i xi. 2— so comm. and Kegcava with offering thirteen different articles; and again : (59. 25), in a rite for universal dominion (comm. ), worshiping Atharvan ; further (note ⁴ to 42. 11), a schol. adds it to vii. 20. 6, to win wealth by Vedic knowledge.     2. Praise and prayer to Indra¶

    Found also in Pâipp. xix. (in the order 1, 3, 2). The second verse is used by Kâug. (29.27) in a remedial rite against demons, while partaking of a rice-mess boiled over birds' nests. Vaâit. (16.13) has the hymn in the agnisfoma, when the soma is tuned into the large wooden vessel.

    Translated : Florenz, 251 or 3; Griffith, i. 245 ; Bloomfield, 66, 458.

    I. For Indra, O priests, press the soma, and add the water; [Indra] who shall hear the praiser's words and my call.

    Or, (the words and call of me the praiser.' With b compare RV. vii. 32.6 d. Ppp.; has, for b, c, (rnotanâ tu dhâvata : stotriyarn: havaṅ crpava” âhavan: tu nak. The comm. regards Z dkâvata as referring to the process called Zâhâvana, performed for the adabkya graka, and refers to ApCS. xii. 8. 2: or, alternatively, to the general purification of the soma. The concluding four syllables of each verse seem like 1 secondary appendages.

    2. Unto whom enter the drops of soma-plant (ânâdkas) as birds a tree; thou exuberant one, drive away the demon-possessed scorners.

    Ppp. reads roâ for yam in a. The comm. takes andṅasas as nom. pl., explaining it Ś by annabhṅras. ¹

    3. Press ye the soma for the soma-drinker, for the thunderbolt-bearing rg Indra ; young, conqueror, lord [is] he, greatly praised.

    The first two pâdas are RV. vii. 32.8 a, b; SV. i. 285 a, b. Ppp. reads in a pâvane, = humoring the meter.

    Ś ( [Atharvan.— nânâdâivatam. I. fathyâbyhati; a. sarstârapankti ; 3. 3-p. virââd gâyatrṅ.] " Found also in Pâipp. xix. Used by Kâug.     Ś Pp y g y t Brahmaṇaspati ; with sons, with brothers, let Aditi now protect our hard ¹ to surpass [and] saving power. e5 The verse is found also in SV.     5. For some one's exaltation¶

    [4rharvan.— âindrâgnam. ânugubham : 2. bhuri.]

    Found also in Pâipp. xix., and in VS. (xvii. 50-52) TS. (iv. 6.37), MS. (il. ro. 3). Used in Kâug. (4.9) in the parvan sacrifice, with an oblation to Agni; and again (59. 7), with vi.6 and vii. 91, by one desiring a village ; and for success in traffic, see under vi. 1. In Vâit. (29. 15) the hymn accompanies the laying on of fuel in the agnicayana, and vs. 2, in the parvan sacrifice (2.145 3. 3), two offerings to Indra; for the use in Vaâit. 16. 9, see under vi. 3. The comm. further points out vs. 2 as addressed to Indra ¹ in the Naks. K. 14. g

    Translated : Ludwig, p. 431; Florenz, 254 or 6; Griffith, i. 247.

    I. Lead him up higher, O Agni, [thou] to whom oblations of ghee are re made ; unite him with splendor, and make him abundant with progeny.

    31. At rising of the sun (or moon)¶

    [Uparibabkrava. —gavyam. gâyatram.]

    Found also in Pâipp. xix., as in RV. (x. 189. I-3), SV. (ii. 726-8), VS. (iii. 6-8), TS. (i. 5.39), K. (vil. 13), MS. (i. 6.1). Used by Kâug. (66.14) in the savayajṅas, with a spotted cow as sava. And by Vâit. in the agnyadheya ceremony (6. 3), as the sacrificer approaches the âkavantya fire; and again in the sattra (33. 28), spoken by the Brahman-priest to the kolar, after the mânasastotra. Also in i. 631-3 = Nâigeyacâkha v. 46-8.

    Translated : as RV. hymn, by Max Miiller, ZDMG. ix. (1855), p- XI ; Geldner, Sâebenzig Lieder des RV, 1875, p. 57; Ludwig, number r6o ; Grassmann, ii. 433; and as AV. hymn, by Florenz, 289 or 41; Griffith, i. 262.

    I. Hither hath stridden this spotted steer, hath sat upon his mother in the east, and going forward to his father, the heaven (suâr).

    All the texts agree in this verse, except that TS, has âsanat and gṅnak in b, while Ppp. has prayat in c. It seems to be a description of the rising of a heavenly body, — the comm. and the translators say, the sun; but the epithet " spotted," and the number thirty in the third verse point rather to the moon. The mother " is of course the earth, upon which it seems to rest a moment.

    2. He moves between the shining spaces, from the breath of this outbreathing [universe]; the bull (Gnakisâ) hath looked forth unto the heaven (svâr).

    RV. (with which, through the whole hymn, SV. and VS. entirely agree) reads (as does TS.) apâanatt (p. apasanatt) at end of b; in c, it reads ââam for svââ. TS. inverts the order of a and b, and has the same c as our text ; on the other hand, MS. has our b, but arpave (for rocanâ) in a, and a wholly peculiar c: grâri wâm skro âhabhik. Ppp. has (nearly as TS.), for a, b, yasya prapâad apanatly antag carati rocanaṅ; and Jivam (with RV.) at the end. The sense of the verse is very obscure, made so by the unintelligible second pada ; Roth suggests apânati as 3d singular J, with 7zocanâ stars as subject : ⁴ They die at his breath ": but this teems with difficulties. [In Geldner's note, anati was taken as 3d plural. ] Our P.M.IR.T.K,, and all SPP's authorities, separate rocanâ asyâ in sa7hîta (the pada-text reading -#7), and SPP. has accordingly, properly enough, adopted it in his text: see the note to Prat. iii. 34. Ppp. also has uyâkkyan. J

    3. Thirty domains (dkâman) he rules over; voice, the bird, hath set up, to meet the day with the lights of morning.

    This translation is one of despair, and of no value, like the others that are given of the verse. Taken by itself, the first pada is well enough, and seems most naturally (as noted above) to refer to the thirty days of the moon's synodical revolution, or spaces of the sky traversed by it in them; to understand it of the thirty divisions of the day (mukâria) looks like an anachronism ; and thirty gods (Ludwig) is wholly senseless. LRoth observes : Ushas, in returning to her point of departure, traverses thirty yejanas (RV. i. 123. 8): the path of the light around the world thus appears to be divided into thirty stages. ] The variety of reading of the texts indicates, as in many other like cases, the perplexity of the text-makers. RY. (with SV.VS.) has, for b, vâ% patamgâya Jhtyare ; TS. and MS. have patargâya, but TS. follows it with (riye, and MS. with hṅyate. Ppp. reads gâya su (riyat. In c, RV. (etc.) reads â4a, particle, for âhas, and the comm. does the same ; TS. gives, for the whole pâda, prâty asya vaka dyubhtk while MS. substitutes our 2 c, in its RV. version, having given its wholly independent version of this as 2 c (see above); Ppp. has at end âivi. In a, MS. reads tâ7câddhâma, as compound ; the other texts (and three of SPP's authorities) have triṅgââd dhâma y (the pada-reading is dhâma). Both TS. and MS., it may be added, put vs. 3 before 2. y

    With this hymn ends the third zzuvâka, of rr hymns and 33 verses ; the extracted { item of Anukr. is simply â72ya (see end of the next annvâka).

    In explaining this verse, the comm. quotes from TB. (i. 1. 246) the legend of the Asuras named kâlakâṅjâ, whose efforts to reach heaven Indra thwarted by a trick, except in the case of two of them, who became the heavenly dogs; a corresponding legend is found in MS. i. 6. 9 (p. ron, L 1 ff.). The different numbers in our hymn, as regards both dog and kâlakâṅjas, are important, and suggest naturally the dog of our sky (Canis major or Sirius: so Zimmer, p. 353) and the three stars of Orion's belt, pointing directly toward it. The Anukr. does not notice the deficiency of a syllable in a.

    I. This one, O ye associates (?sadkâstha), I deliver to you, whom Jatavedas shall carry [as] a treasure; the sacrificer follows after well-being ; him do ye recognize in the highest firmament.

    The verse is found also in VS. (xviii. 59) and K. (xl. 13). VS. reads, in a, saâhastha and ze (for vas); in b, it puts gevadhim after auâhatz ; in c, it reads a. yajRâpatir vo âtra.     2. Recognize ye him in the highest firmament; ye divine associates, ye know [his] world there; the sacrificer follows after well-being; make ye what he has offered and bestowed plain for him.

    This verse is found with the preceding in VS. (xviii. 60) and K. (xl. 13), and also in TB. (iil. 7. 133-4), but with considerable variants : at the beginning, e247 janâtha (TB. Jantar) par-; in b, TB. vFṅas for das, both VS. and TB. sadk- unaccented, which is better, but VS. vida, which is bad, and both râpâm asya (for lokâm âira), which gives a better sense ; for c, both yâd agâchar pathtbhir devayânâis; in d, both iszâp arte, and VS. krnavârka, but TB. kr nuat, both without sa.

    3. O gods! O Fathers ! O Fathers! O gods I who I am, he am I.

    The comm., with his usual carelessness of accent, takes the vocatives here for nominatives. Some of SPP's authorities (also our O.s.m.) omit the accent of the first asi.

    4. He do I cook, he do I give, he do I offer; [as] he, let me not be parted from what is given.

    That is, from my gifts, or their reward. The comm. counts and explains these two quasi-verses, 3 and 4, as one. But the Anukr. reckons this hymn (as it reckoned the preceding one) as one of five verses (paṅcarca), and SPP's edition as well as ours so divides. ]'As that one, I cook ' etc. would be an equally accurate translation, and the English of it is not so harsh.

    5. In the firmament, O king, stand firm ; there let this stand firm; know of what we have bestowed, O king; do thou, O god, be well-willing.

    The comm. understands the addresses of this verse as made to Soma, which is very questionable ; and the « this" of b to be the ââpârtdâm, which (or dattâm, vs. 4) is right. It must be by a corruption of the text that the Anukr. does not define the verse as an anustubk.

    128. For auspicious time : with dung-smoke¶

    [Atharvâṅgiras (naksatrarâjânar: candramasam astâut).— caturycam. sâumyam ; akadkâmadevatyam. ânustubham.]

    Except the third verse, this hymn occurs also in Pâipp. xix. Besides the ceremony reported under vs. r, Kâug. has the hymn (50. 13) in a general rite for good fortune, with vi. I, 3-7, 59, etc. etc.; and also, in the chapter of portents (roo. 3), in an expiatory ceremony on occasion of an eclipse of the moon (somagrakaṇa, comm); vs. 3, too, is specially quoted in the as7akâ ceremony (138.8), as accompanying a nineteenth [oblation 7].

    Translated : Weber, Omina und Portenta (1858), p. 363 ; Zimmer, p. 353 ; Griffith, i. 316 ; Bloomfield, 16o, 532.— Bloomfield bad already treated it at length, AJP. vii. 484 ff, and JAOS. xiii. p. cxxxiii (= PAOS., Oct. 1886). A pencilled note on Whitney's ms. shows that he considered the propriety of rewriting the tranŚlation and comment for vi. 128,

    I. When the asterisms made the gakadkâma their king, they bestowed on him auspicious (54adyâ-) day, saying " This shall be [his] royalty."

    Cakadhtma (with irregular but not unparalleled accent: see my Skz. Gr. § 1267 b) means primarily ' dung-smoke,' i.e. smoke arising from burning dung (or else the vapor from fresh dung). According to the comm., it signifies here the fire from which such smoke arises, and then, on account of inseparability from that, a Brahman "; and he quotes TS. v. 2, 8r-2 : “a Brahman is indeed this Agni Vâigvânara." The Kaâugika- Sttra, in a passage (50.15, 16) also quoted by the comm., says that, with this hymn, having laid balls of dung on the joints of a Brahman friend, one asks dung-smoke, «what sort of day today?" He (of course, the Brahman ) answers « propitious, very favorable." ' Prof. Bloomfield takes gakaâkṅma to be out-and-out the title of a Brahman, " weather-prophet " ; but this seems not to follow from the Stra, also not from the Anukr., and least of all from the hymn. The Pâipp. version differs considerably from ours (but nearly agrees with one in an appendix to the Nakgatra-kalpa : see Bloomfield, AIP. vii. 485) : it reads yad râzjâanaṅz cakadkâman naksarrâṇy aky pura : Śhadrakam asmâi prâ 'yachan taro rastram ajâyata. The accentof âsâr ind is not explained by any known rule. So Kegava to Kâug. so. 16.

    2. Auspicious day ours at noon, auspicious day be ours at evening, auspicious day ours in the morning of the days ; be night auspicious day for us.

    That is, may each of these times be free from omens and influences of ill-luck. The Ppp. version runs thus : 54. astu nas sayaṅ: bk. prâtar astu nak : bk. asmabhyark tvar cakadhuma sada krpun (as in the appendix to the Naksatra-kalpa just cited).

    3. From day-and-night, from the asterisms, from sun-and-moon; do thou, O king gaṅadhṅma, make auspicious day for us.

    This verse, as already noted, is wanting in Ppp., but its second half nearly agrees with that of the Ppp. version of vs. 2. The accent in b should be emended to sâryâcan- âramasâbhyâm, as is read below I see W's note] in xi.3. 34. The first halfverse is metrically irregular. = ¹ 4. Thou who hast made auspicious day for us at evening, by night, ¹l also by day —to thee as such, O gakadhṅma, king of the asterisms, [be] bx always homage. ¹ Ppp. reads akarar at end of a, and prâtar for naktam in b.     2. With the red knife (svââhâti), make thou a pair (mithunâ) on

    [their] two ears; the Agvins have made the mark ; be that numerous by 1 The comm. explains mithunam as stṅuṅsâtmakan ciknam, and regards it as = applied to the calf's ears. [If the comm. is correct on this point, as is altogether likely, this marking the cattle's ears with marks resembling the genitals is a bit of symbolism most interesting to the student of folk-lore.] The red ' knife is doubtless of copper I so also the comm.     3. As the gods and Asuras made [it], as human beings also, so, O Agvins, make ye the mark, in order to thousand-fold prosperity.

    142. For increase of barley¶

    [ Vicuâmitra.— vâyavyam. ânugtubham.]

    Not found in Pâipp. Used by Kâug. (24. 1) in a rite of preparation for sowing seed, and reckoned (19. I, note) among the pustika mantras; vs. 3 also appears (19. 27) in a rite for prosperity, with binding on an amulet of barley.

    Translated : Ludwig, p. 463 ; Zimmer, p. 237; Grill, 66, 1775 Griffith, i. 324 ; Bloomfield, 141, 54T.— See also Bergaigne-Henry, Manuel, p. 156.

    I. Rise up (uṅ-gri), become abundant (Śakâ) with thine own greatness, O barley ; ruin (77) all receptacles ; let not the bolt from heaven smite thee.

    Instead of my n7ki in c, the comm. reads v7 niki, which he says is, ' by letter-substitution,' for py nîki 'fill'¹ Prp7hi would be an easier reading, and was conjectured by Ludwig, and before him by Aufrecht (KZ. xxvii. 218). [Griffith and Bl, ' fill them till they burst." ]

    2. Where we appeal unto thee, the divine barley that listens, there (²41) rise up, like the sky; be unexhausted, like the ocean. The comm., in b, reads fa(ra and achavad-.

    3. Unexhausted be thine attendants (?upasâd), unexhausted thy heaps; thy bestowers be unexhausted ; thy eaters be unexhausted.

    The comm. explains upasadas as here rendered (supagantârak karmakarâṇ) ; the translators conjecture ⁴ piles,' a meaning which cannot properly be found in the word.

    By a strangely unequal division, the thirteenth and last zzuvâka is made to consist of 18 hymns and 64 verses ; the quoted Anukr. says yak parak sa catuhsastil.

    The fifteenth prapâthaka ends with the book.

    Some of the mss. sum up the book correctly as containing 142 hymns and 454 verses.
